* `exfat_hosttest.c` links `rach2/sys/fs/exfat.c` and `fatcommon.c` over `rach2/sys/fs/bcache.c` and the disk model of `compat/devmodel.c`, on volumes `mkexfatfixture` builds. Rows read the well-formed volume back through every shape of file and entry set, with each of three forms of up-case table; refuse each damaged volume at mount, mount it read-only, skip the set or fail at the file, naming the reason; refuse a volume of another type with no record; mount a volume another implementation wrote with `mkexfat_external.sh`; run fourteen write sequences, then stop each after every write, judge every state the model can leave and fail each barrier in turn; bound the cost of creates in a large directory; check durability; count a write and an `fsync` beside a neighbour of 64 dirty blocks and judge a stop after that `fsync`; rename a set of the format's 256 entries, which a longer name would take past 256; and mount dirty volumes. Its ioctl-entry row admits a device with a retained inode by its operations flag while keeping the last release and unmarked operations locked.

### Buses, storage and block devices

* `pcimsi_hosttest.c` runs `pci_msi_enable`, `pci_msi_disable` and the three MSI-X calls of `rach2/sys/pci.c`, with the accessors of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/pciconf.c`, over a model of configuration space behind ports 0xCF8 and 0xCFC that records every write in order. MSI rows check 32-bit, 64-bit and masking capabilities, the enable bit cleared first and set last, an enabled MSI-X disabled first, and the refusals. MSI-X rows check entry 0, the table written between the masked enable and the unmasking write, a hold, and the refusals. A halfword write must leave the status word beside it unchanged, and every access the interrupt flag. With `rach2/sys/arch/x86/vector.c`, the pool rows take all 191 message vectors, past the sixteen of the old pool, never 0x80, refuse the 192nd, give vectors back and take them again lowest first, and take aligned ones.
* `pciwalk_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/pci.c` whole, with the accessors of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/pciconf.c`, over a model of configuration space with an empty slot, a function of vendor 0x0000, a multi-function device with a gap, a single-function device that answers for all eight functions, an unnumbered bridge, a chain of two bridges, a bridge to a bus numbered below its own, a CardBus bridge, a second root bus and a bus no bridge leads to, and a model where a bridge leads to a bus numbered below its own. It checks that the walk lists every function a bridge leads to or a root bus has, in the order of bus, device and function, reads device 0 of every other bus once, and is not made again by a later search, which reads the nine dwords of the function it returns as they are then; each finder over that list; a list of 240 functions that moves to the heap, and stops with a warning when the heap refuses; and, on a model whose BARs decode what a row gives them, the sizes the walk finds for 32-bit, 64-bit, prefetchable, I/O and unused BARs, a BAR of 8 GiB and the ROMs of a function and of a bridge, the resources `pci_resource` gives as Linux's `/sys` shows them, every BAR's value put back, and decoding off while each BAR is sized but on a host bridge.
* `vtd_hosttest.c` includes the VT-d driver of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/iommu/`, links `rach2/sys/pci.c` and `pciconf.c` over a configuration space in memory, and runs them against a model of the remapping units built from QEMU's DMAR table and the Lenovo's. The model has the units' caches, caching mode, and a copy of memory that only a write-back updates for a unit whose walks are not coherent, and counts each command the VT-d specification forbids. [The driver's Tests section](../../rach2/sys/arch/x86/iommu/Readme.md#tests) lists the rows.
* `ahci_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/ahci.c` over a model controller, a register array behind `struct ahci_io` whose state moves on as the driver writes. Bring-up rows run one step each: resets and firmware handoffs that complete or never do, and ports with a disk, with nothing, with a link that comes up on reset, with another device, or none at all. Link rows count the delays of the link's debounce, in which the model's time passes: an empty port beside a disk settles with it after 100 ms of polls and is stopped, a DET that stays 1 waits out the 2 s deadline, as Linux's `sata_link_debounce`, and a DET that still changes one poll past it fails the try, which is tried again after the try's deadline at a lower speed, as Linux's `ata_eh_reset`: a link that never holds is dropped after four tries at 1.5 Gb/s, and one that holds only at 1.5 Gb/s is found on the third; two ports reset together before a signature is read, and on a controller with staggered spin-up the second only after the first's signature. Command rows cover completion, waits of thirty seconds with interrupts on and five with them off, a short transfer, a fatal status, the disk policy, a 4096-byte logical sector, a 2048-sector read in 257 table entries, and buffers whose modeled physical pages lie above 4 GiB on 64-bit and 32-bit controllers, independently of the host virtual addresses. Interrupt rows check completions inside the command-issue write, the queue restarted after a polled command, and a panic before DMA state is released when the engine will not stop. A held read or write flush on port zero stays active while an explicit answer on port one completes that port's request, with its own generation and status; only port zero's answer completes the held request. Port-lock rows check that a request submitted while a polled command has claimed the port is queued with nothing issued and the lock given back, that the interrupt leaves the claimed port's status to its caller, that the claim's release issues the queued request with the port's lock held and its interrupt completes it, and that a second polled command waits out the first's claim and issues under its own with the lock let go. Claim-deadline rows check that a caller sleeping for the port, which a third caller claims again before the sleeper runs, keeps one deadline across its sleeps and fails with `EIO` at its bound, the other claim untouched and its own timer restored, and that a sleep which took most of the bound leaves the command only the rest.
* `ata_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/ata.c` and gives it a model drive through `struct ata_io`, whose phase moves on after a number of status reads, when the driver pauses off the device, or when the channel's owner sleeps, in which the test runs another caller. Rows cover the owner's DMA read with a polled read inside its sleep, which must wait out the DMA and read its own sector; a drive that never answers; data-port writes with interrupts off; a failed DMA; a slave's flush after the master was selected; the scheduler stopped; the thirty-second sleeping wait; four ticks counted in `disk-wait-ticks` while the owner sleeps, three of them by one call of `ata_tick`, as after boot ticks that found the big lock held; a sleep that begins two ticks into a skipped span, a wait that ends two ticks after the last call, and a timer interrupt the model delivers at the driver's read of `kernel_ticks`, held while interrupts are masked, as a wait reads its start; a 4096-byte logical sector in IDENTIFY; DMA writes of 1, 8, 64 and 72 sectors at 28-bit and 48-bit addresses; writes durable at return; the interrupt, the tick and a sleeper's test with the channel's lock held, each of which must return without reading the status, the tick leaving its count to its next call; and the interrupt's registration with `ISR_UNLOCKED`. The owner's read and the polled read inside its sleep must each leave the channel's lock free, which the host's lock checks, and the host's lock traps a path that takes a held lock rather than trying it.
* `nvme_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/nvme.c` and gives it a model controller, reached through `struct nvme_io`, that reads the queues the driver rings for, answers admin commands, and moves data through the driver's PRP entries, finding each host byte by its address. Rows cover bring-up and the `nvme` disk it registers; refusals by name of a controller never ready, with a fatal status, without the NVM command set, with an 8 KiB smallest page or without memory, and of namespaces with 4096-byte blocks or metadata; PRP lists over scattered pages; splits at MDTS; the bounce page; failed and stray completions; timeouts, recovery and a dead controller, with every slot free after them; a polled command answered during its wait's last delay, which resets nothing, one that another caller's recovery fails, whose slot stays taken until it frees it, and one whose wait runs out during another's recovery, which waits that recovery out; a controller that will not stop; ten requests on eight slots; FUA and the flush mark; seventeen namespaces; and the fallbacks to MSI and to polling. The queued controller's handler must be registered `ISR_UNLOCKED`, and its queue's locks must be free between calls.
* `blank_hosttest.c` includes the console's blank, `rach2/sys/display/blank.c`, with the screen, the backlights, the log and the tick as records. Rows check that `consoleblank=` sets the time; that the text console blanks once the time has passed since the last poke, the screen and the backlights together with a record, and not a tick before, and that a poke shows both again at the next tick; that while a DRM master holds the screen neither the countdown past the time, a forced blank nor an unblank changes the screen or a backlight or writes a record; that once the console has the screen back and the countdown starts again it blanks at the time; and that a program's `/fb` keeps it from blanking.
* `block_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/block.c` and runs its partition view over a 1000-sector backing device that records each request. A 200-sector view at LBA 100 forwards in-range reads and writes with its base added and hands a flush on; a request past its end or of zero sectors fails without reaching the backing. A view that starts past the device is empty, and one that runs past it is cut short. `block_physical` finds the backing under a view and under a view of a view, and takes a loop device's record for no view.
* `lag_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/lag.c`, the test device whose requests complete from the timer `rach2.blocklag` ticks later, here three. Its backing device enters the wrapper again, or runs the timer, from inside its own write, as a caller asleep in the driver lets another do. Rows require that `rach2.blocklag` and `rach2.blockfail` refuse a value with no digits, a trailing non-digit, zero, or one past a million or past 32 bits, so that the wrapper is not installed or no write is armed, that a later wrap of another device returns that device unchanged and one of the wrapped device returns the wrapper, that a slot whose transfer has not returned is not delivered, that two overlapping requests are each answered once after the lag and once with the duplicate's error, that a read's page stays scrubbed until its answer, that a flush takes a slot, that one call of the timer after the lag's ticks answers a request and one after two units more its duplicate, that a request taken two ticks into ticks the timer skipped is answered at its own lag and not a tick before, and that every slot is free at the end.
* `request_hosttest.c` compiles the real block request pool, partition views and lag queue with a fixed allocator that reuses freed addresses, and a model heap of six runs of up to 32 pages whose longest run a row may shorten. Explicit lag answers and request ticks check shared ownership across partitions, independent completion, private read and write pages, flushes, wrong generations, duplicates before caller release and after address reuse, quarantine, and late release. The backing model also tries to deliver while a transfer is unfinished. Each tick advances the test's `kernel_ticks`, by which the clocks count. Deadline rows advance only the request clock: an answer before the first deadline, at each nonfinal deadline, or one tick before the final deadline succeeds, including across tick wrap. One call of `block_request_tick` after four ticks counts four wait ticks, and three calls over eight ticks pass a deadline of eight, as the boot processor's tick counts after ticks that found the big lock held; a request taken three ticks into such a span counts one wait tick at the next call and passes its deadline eight ticks after it was taken, and one answered two ticks after the last call counts them at its completion. A flush answered after one period succeeds; one at the final deadline returns EIO and retains its DMA page until the late answer. A third device waits for the two-slot pool and receives a fresh deadline at admission. The latency record measures the pool wait and the device wait from their distinct start events under the same logical clock. A model big lock counts its takes: the lag device is written and flushed with no take; a device is read, flushed and read through a view with no take, its submit called with neither the big lock nor interrupts off, its caller waiting on its own request, and a completion with interrupts off and no big lock ending the wait; a caller that holds the lock calls the device holding it, with no further take. The kept-buffer rows follow Linux 6.12.115's mempool: a read of two pages takes a new buffer of 32 pages, kept when its request is freed; a read of three pages takes the kept buffer and asks the heap for nothing, and a read of one page takes a DMA frame instead; of five requests freed, four keep their buffers and the fifth gives its back, as `mempool_free` keeps an element only below the minimum; the buffer kept last is taken first; the shrinker gets all four back, 128 pages, though reclaim asked for one, and nothing once none is kept; with no run of 32 pages a read of three pages takes three, which go back to the heap when it is freed; and a read the heap cannot hold even at its own length is refused and frees its record. The frame rows read into four model page frames with counted references, as `block_read_frames` reads a window of the file page cache on a device with `BLOCK_FRAMES`: a completed read lands in the caller's frames with no buffer and gives back its list of frames, its record and its reference on each frame; a read quarantined at its last deadline fails with EIO and keeps its references after the caller lets go of the frames, so the device's late transfer lands in frames no one else has, while the stalled device admits no other request; the late answer frees the old frames, the list and the record and clears the stall, and the read again goes into new frames. The rows of reads started without a waiter (`block_read_frames_async`) answer a read at the tick's log of its quarantine, after the tick lets go of the pool's lock and before it calls the completions it claimed, and the tick still calls the completion once, with EIO, and the read gives back its frames, list and record; a read answered after the tick has its completion called once, at the tick, and keeps its frames until the answer. A read of sector 80 through a view at 2048, which reaches the device at 2128, makes a settle of the view's sectors 80 to 87 wait, and a settle through the device of 2135 wait for a read of the view's 84 to 91, while settles of sectors next to them, in the view's numbers or the device's, go on. No assertion uses elapsed time.
* `bcache_hosttest.c` runs the buffer cache, `rach2/sys/fs/bcache.c` with `rach2/sys/block/chain.c`, over fake polled devices in memory; no row may reach the sleeping wait. Rows cover hits, misses and blank gets; a short last block, a block past the device and one whose sector would wrap; the least recently released victim; invalidation refused while a buffer is held or dirty; syncs that write blocks marked first before the rest, and the rest in block order; a failed write kept dirty and recorded; torn and dropped writes, which the cache cannot see; a device gone during a read or a write; runs sized to the device's requests and half the cache; a write-through disk flushed before an `after` and once at a sync's end; an owned sync's writes counted beside a large dirty neighbour, which stays dirty, with no flush; a shared block's write alone, the firsts marked before a shared or owned `after` ahead of it and those marked after it left; a first dirtied during a sync written before an `after` marked after it and otherwise left to the next sync; the blocks a device writes last, which a failed write keeps dirty and from an eviction until it heals, with an `after` while one of them is a `first`; a failed data write keeping its table block and the free's bitmap behind it off the device, a drain into a table block during a sync's last pass having its data written first, a table block's write taking only the blocks it may name, and a writer that dirties a block at every write not keeping a sync; pointer blocks written after their data and below the blocks above them whatever their numbers, not after data dirtied later or another owner's, a shared tree apart from a file's, kept off the device by a failed data write, passed over by a shrink while they wait, a chain from an `after` through a `first` pointer block to its data, and a leaf again after an owned mark; a reserve's writes, after which the misses it asked for write nothing; those blocks after a failed write, which a shrink, an eviction or a reserve does not write ahead of an `after` or in a run, and a sync still retries; and a sort of more than 4096 blocks, at whose service point a `first` dirtied behind the sync's look is seen, so that the batch is gathered again and the plain blocks reach the device before that `first`, and the `after` and the block written last follow it, and a block written there goes once; a release's wake of a buffer's queue, made before `cache_spin` goes and a shrink run at that moment frees the buffer whose waiter left, and no wake for a buffer nobody waits for. A broken hold rule must panic in a forked child. The test counts the takes of the cache's write lock, `wb_lock`, and requires every call to give it back. The reader rows stand for a caller without the big lock: a hit takes no lock; a miss that finds a clean buffer near the head of the release order chooses and keys it and reads the device with no lock, and writes nothing; and a miss among dirty buffers takes the write lock before it chooses, to write one; none takes the big lock. An argument that begins with `r` runs the reader rows alone. `victim_dirty_and_irq_rows` dirties an unbarred buffer at each of sixty pauses behind a victim search, beyond its sixteen idle retries, and requires the search to write and take it once the hits stop. With every buffer held and interrupts off, another miss must exhaust its retries without a service point; the interrupt-flag model makes a direct service point fail that row, while `sched_give_way` skips it. The unmount's drop, `bcache_drop`, must take a device that an invalidate refuses for a dirty and a held buffer: the dirty one dropped unwritten and counted, the held one kept until its release drops it, and the failed-write mark cleared.
* `bcache_cost_hosttest.c` runs the same sources and bounds `bcache_stat_visits`, the count of buffers the cache's list walks read, on a cache of 1024 buffers. A get and its release read at most eight, whether a hit with three buffers held or a miss on the full cache. A sync of 1024 dirty blocks reads at most four walks of the cache plus eight reads and twenty heap steps a block, or twelve walks when the heap grants only a page and it gathers batches of 128; a sync of eight dirty blocks reads those eight. Each bound has a floor, so a counter that counts nothing fails. The cache must stop at half the heap's pages: 32768 for a 256 MiB region, 8192 for a quarter of it.
* `bcache_lock_hosttest.c` runs the buffer cache with the kernel's processes as host threads, its write lock a mutex the threads share and the big lock never held, against a polled device of 256 blocks whose writes and reads give the processor up first and whose blocks written last, a callback that gives it up too, let other threads run inside every walk of the dirty lists. Each block holds a version written in two halves with a yield between. Writers dirty blocks in each of the cache's ways, plain, ordered, owned, pointer and written through, and syncers sync the device, its unflushed and owned blocks and single blocks, on a cache cut to 48 buffers, in four rows: four writers and two syncers on every block, one writer and three syncers on one block, three writers and a syncer beside two threads that reserve buffers, and two writers and two syncers beside two shrinkers that reclaim clean buffers and write dirty victims. A writer must find the version it last stored, a sync must leave every version released before it on the device, a reserve may grant no more than it asked, and at the end the device must hold every version, the dirty lists must be whole and empty, and no write may be torn, no failure recorded and no big lock taken. Two initial rows force clean reclaim at the last release of a two-block write run and at a writing shrink's release: the run's last block must already be saved, and the shrink's reference must prevent reclaim until its last look. AddressSanitizer detects either old path's read of freed storage. With `RACH2_BCACHE_NO_WB` set the write lock is not taken, and each run fails these checks and stops on a walk of a dirty list that goes round for ever.
* `mbr_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/mbr.c`, whose functions are pure, and hands them tables built in a buffer. `mbr_parse` refuses a sector without the signature. `mbr_bootpart_pick` takes the GRUB disk's shape, a FAT partition (type 0x0C, 0x0B or 0xEF) beside KRFFS, and refuses each fact changed in turn: the type, a start at 0, an empty entry, a bad index, a range past the disk or one whose 32-bit end wraps, and an overlap with KRFFS. Other rows check `mbr_bootpart_ok`, `mbr_gap_clear` over LBA 1 to 2047, and `mbr_take`, which counts the entries it refuses.
* `gpt_hosttest.c` runs `rach2/sys/block/gpt.c` over tables built byte by byte, the entry array once in a buffer of its exact length. Rows check the protective MBR; a sound header and a checksum over the whole array; a header with a bad checksum; a primary header at the backup's sector, refused, and a backup at its own, taken; an entry count that would overflow; entry sizes either side of the 128-byte minimum; a usable range or array past the device; and entries passed over and counted for overlapping, lying outside the usable range, ending before they begin, passing a ten-byte read's reach, or exceeding the four kept.
* `partition_hosttest.c` runs `rach2/sys/block/partition.c`, with `gpt.c` and `mbr.c`, over a 4096-sector disk in memory. `part_scan` reads an MBR with three logical partitions in an extended one, a chain that loops, a logical partition past its extended one, a GUID table of six entries with their GUIDs and labels, one read from its backup when the primary header or array is damaged, one with both damaged, a blank disk, an unreadable sector 0, and more entries than `PART_MAX`. `part_gpt_as_mbr` gives the installed layout as four MBR records, and `part_bios_boot` finds its BIOS boot partition as the window of `/dev/grubcore`, refusing an entry over sector 0 or either header or array.
* `diskcmd_hosttest.c` runs `rach2/sys/block/diskcmd.c`, the table of ATA commands each build policy permits, through `diskcmd_allowed_under`, which takes the policy as an argument. All 256 command bytes are offered under each policy: read-write permits every one, and read-only exactly five, the four read commands and IDENTIFY. Named rows refuse the writes and flushes, unknown commands, and a write under a policy value that is neither, and the banner must name the policy `diskcmd_policy` returns. `tools/inspect/check_diskpolicy.sh` checks the policy of the built kernel.
* `diskinfo_hosttest.c` runs `rach2/sys/block/diskinfo.c`, with `mbr.c`, `partition.c` and `gpt.c`, over IDENTIFY words and disk records built in memory. Rows check ATA strings, byte-swapped within each word and unterminated, with control bytes shown as `?`; `diskinfo_u64_dec`, which prints a 48-bit count in decimal over 32-bit halves; the `/proc/disk` text of a kernel never asked, one with no device, the read-only policy and a present disk, line by line, and its truncation; and the `disk` and `identity` lines of two disks, a removed disk hidden while its mount holds it.
* `xhci_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/usb/xhci.c` and gives it a model controller through `struct xhci_io`, with registers at offsets the driver must read. Bring-up rows cover discovery (apertures in I/O space, never programmed, or above 4 GiB, mapped whole where pointers are 64 bits and refused where they are 32; a controller in D3), an EHCI released from its firmware, the capability registers and list, a firmware handoff answered, slow or never answered, resets and readiness that never come, and scratchpad pages. Later rows cover the command and event rings, commands never answered and aborted, ports, slot and endpoint commands with route strings and translators, Intel port routing, interrupts by MSI and by line, transfers and their recovery, and the DMA pool without AC64.
* `usb_hosttest.c` includes the USB core, `rach2/sys/usb/core.c`, over a model of the xHCI command calls that keeps each endpoint state's preconditions. It walks configuration descriptors in buffers of their exact length: a boot keyboard found, an unknown descriptor stepped over, interfaces refused for their class, setting, configuration or endpoint, malformed lengths ending the walk, and storage, HID and hub candidates taken only when complete. Other rows run an endpoint's quiesce by Stop Endpoint and Set TR Dequeue under the enumeration turn, with its failures and deadlines; a failed or stalled report; Disable Slot and its tombstones; and Enable Slot timeouts. HID rows read a HID descriptor's bcdHID and report descriptor length, and take an interface of any subclass and protocol. LED rows step a running keyboard: SET_REPORT of the Output report to its interface with a one-byte OUT data stage holding the byte the HID driver offers, one at a time, the outcome told to the HID driver on its completion, a stall or no completion within `STEP_MS` told as a failure, a transfer the controller refuses offered again at the next step, and a mouse's driver asked too. Multi-interface rows walk configurations of two and three HID interfaces, past a vendor's interface and an alternate setting and up to the room given, and enumerate them from the whole configuration to their running over a model that answers every command and request: each interface's endpoint opened, SET_IDLE and the report descriptor sent to each interface's number, each interface's report transfer, a completion handed on for the interface of its endpoint, an output report sent to the interface that offered it, an interface that refuses its report descriptor given up alone while the next is set up, and a device of one such interface failed.
* `usb_interrupt_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/usb/usb.c` and runs `usb_interrupts_start` and the pump, `usb_poll`, over stubs of up to four controllers, each on MSI, a PCI line or polling, with the interrupt flag modelled. A pending source must keep the fallback count at zero and the flag as it was; work with no pending source counts once, and an empty drain not at all. Rows also cover mixed modes, failed enables left uninspected, the vector of a failed MSI enable given back with its handler and taken by the next controller, a message seen at the second check, polling with no interrupt configured, and a poll after a gap of five ticks, which must pass their milliseconds to the controllers and the core.
* `usbstamp_hosttest.c` includes the USB latency recorder, `rach2/sys/usb/stamp.c`, and the scheduler, `rach2/sys/proc/sched.c`, with the clock, interrupt mask and processor switch replaced, and drives one event from the pump to the reader's dispatch. It checks that a slow read stays recorded with its times, that the reader's dispatch freezes the work counts and another process's does not, that the count saturates, that a read consumes the record, that a code taken after the reader's dispatch leaves the keystroke's record, and that a disabled recorder or an eventless pump records nothing. Given a mode, it prints a record for the `latency-checker` case of `tests/run usb`, whose program is `tests/cases/host/usbchecks.c`.
* `hub_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/usb/core.c` and `rach2/sys/usb/hub.c` over a model controller whose devices answer by location, and a hub that answers its class requests from a port table each row sets. Rows cover USB 2 and SuperSpeed hubs (descriptor, ports, depth, translators, think time); ganged, per-port and unswitched power; devices found by a scan, by the change endpoint, or during a scan; resets that never end; USB 3 link changes and warm resets; over-current; disconnection, and a pulled hub closing its children first; a detached hub kept until Disable Slot succeeds; twenty ports, the twentieth with 15 in its route nibble; long configurations; a full device table; no free slot; an Address Device refused and tried again after a port reset on the same slot, four times in all; the location and the count of a keyboard's reports `/proc/usb` gives; and the stack counted busy from a hub's arrival until its scan ends and its devices run.
* `hid_hosttest.c` includes the HID class driver, `rach2/sys/usb/hid.c`, over a model of the parser and the usage mapping, and records the key events it gives the input layer as the set-1 bytes of each key, with a model of the input layer that keeps each keyboard's held keys. Keyboard rows cover a key pressed, held and released, two keys at once, modifiers, right-hand modifiers and arrows in their two-byte form, Print Screen and Pause with their usages as `MSC_SCAN`, a rollover report whose keys are dropped and whose modifiers are read, a short report, a device gone with keys down, and a second keyboard's first report. Boot keyboards without strings are `USB HIDBP Keyboard 0627:0001`. Mouse rows check the event device registered once as Linux's usbmouse names it, `USB HIDBP Mouse 0627:0001`, with a wheel in 120ths and the side buttons; the movement, the vertical axis as the report has it, the five buttons, the wheel's byte and a report of three bytes without one, a short report dropped, and the device taken away once when the mouse goes. LED rows check each lock at its bit of the output report; a report offered when a keyboard is attached and again only when the LEDs wanted change; none after a failure, which is logged once, until the keyboard is attached again; and none for a mouse, a device never attached or one out of range. Report-protocol rows check the name usbhid gives, from the strings or `HID 0627:0001` without them, the place and version; a device taken once, its report size, its reports passed whole to the mapping and its disconnect when it goes; the multiplier report before the LEDs, its failure passed on and not sent again; and a refused descriptor, or one with no input device, leaving the device to the boot protocol. Two-interface rows take a keyboard whose consumer keys are its second interface: a boot keyboard on interface 0 and a report-protocol interface 1, each with its phys, `usb-4/input0` and `usb-4/input1`, a report on each going to its own, the LEDs only to the keyboard's, no interface past the room for them, and both taken away when the keyboard goes.
* `hid_parse_hosttest.c` includes the report descriptor parser, `rach2/sys/usb/hid_parse.c`, and the usage mapping, `rach2/sys/usb/hid_input.c`, over a model of the event devices that drops what Linux's input core drops before a reader sees it. Parser rows read QEMU's tablet, mouse and keyboard descriptors, field by field, and a keyboard with numbered Consumer and System Control applications; refuse an empty descriptor, an item cut short, a long item, a Report Size of 257, a Report Count of 12289, Report IDs 0 and 256, an End Collection or a Pop with nothing open, a descriptor ending in a collection or a delimiter, a fifth Push, a logical maximum below the minimum, global item 12, a report over 16383 bytes, and unbalanced and nested delimiters; and cut a usage range at 12288 usages and a report at 256 fields. Mapping rows check, against what Linux 6.12 gives the same descriptors, each device's name, IDs, keys, axes and their limits, LEDs and `MSC_SCAN`; the tablet's moves, clicks, wheel and a short report; the mouse's motion and its packet in the mouse queue; the keyboard's 163 keys, its modifiers, an array's presses and releases and ErrorRollOver; the Consumer and System Controls joining the keyboard, AC Pan and a report of an unknown ID; two applications named by their suffixes; a vendor application making none; the LED reports with and without an ID; and a Resolution Multiplier's report and the wheel's fractions once it is set.
* `input_hosttest.c` includes the keyboards' stream, `rach2/sys/input.c`, with `kbd.c`'s tables, and records what the event devices, the console's keyboard and its raw view, `kd_raw_code`, take, the tick's count advanced by the rows. Rows check both Shift keys alone and together on one keyboard and across two, each side of Ctrl, Alt and Super, a press of a held key and a release of a free one dropped, each keyboard's set-1 prefix kept apart, a PS/2 keyboard's own repeat dropped, Pause's sequence; the raw view of a press, a repeat, a release, an extended key, Print Screen and Pause; the repeat by ticks and counts, 31 repeats in 1250 ms from the accumulated deadlines, a modifier or a lock neither repeating nor stopping it, another key taking it, its stop at release, at `input_cancel_repeat` and at a keyboard's detach, a late tick, a set delay and a period of 0; the repeat and the LEDs told to each keyboard's event device; a grabbed keyboard's keys kept from the console; and the events written to a keyboard's event device, a key reaching the console and its raw view, an LED lit and put out on every keyboard, and the repeat's delay and period set.
* `mqueue_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/ipc/mqueue.c` and calls `mq_deadline` and `mq_sleep` over a model clock whose setting moves its time and step count. Rows check that a timed wait on real time follows a setting of the clock: a step back during the wait counts its tick again and sleeps on, a step forward past the time ends it with `ETIMEDOUT`, a step between the reading of the deadline and the sleep is counted before the sleep, and the wait keeps the step count for the tick's wake; and a wait whose tick comes before the clock reaches its time counts its tick again and sleeps on.
* `msc_hosttest.c` includes the Bulk-Only mass-storage driver, `rach2/sys/usb/msc.c`, over a model stick, a SCSI target with a knob for each case, whose transfers a pump hands back as the USB core's does. Bring-up rows cover a ready stick, one that fails TEST UNIT READY twelve times, one never ready, a stalled Get Max LUN, a CD-ROM unit before the disk, card readers, serials, a capacity past a ten-byte read, 4 KiB blocks, write protection, ten seconds for each unit to start, and a stick counted as starting until it is ready or refused. Transfer rows cover reads of up to 1024 sectors, writes, flushes, short reads, unit attentions, status wrappers with the wrong tag or stalled, transfers and recoveries never answered, and a stick pulled in each stage, its pages untouched until its slot is given back. A read handed over by a caller without the big lock must be admitted under one take of it, which the driver gives back; one handed over while another processor holds the lock must wait in the intake, with no take, until the next pump pass admits and sends it; and one admitted while the stick recovers must wait in the queue until the recovery ends.
* `usbdisk_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/block/usbdisk.c` and links the disk registry, `rach2/sys/block/disk.c`, with `partition.c` and `gpt.c`; the class driver, request layer, devfs and caches are stubs, and a medium in memory answers one request a pump pass. Rows check that a stick becomes removable disk 0 with its table read and its views published; that a pull hides it at once and reclaims it at its last hold; that the next stick takes number 0 again; that ten plugs and pulls free every record; that a stick pulled mid-scan is reclaimed; that two units are two disks; that an arrival during a system call is left to the call's entry; and that `disk_register` refuses 2^55 sectors.

### The platform, display and sound

* `acpi_hosttest.c` runs the ACPI table parser and the `\_S5` search of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/acpi.c` over a model of physical memory, a few buffers at physical addresses, which also answers `map_mmio`. Into it go the tables QEMU's SeaBIOS left for `-machine pc` and `-machine q35`, in `acpi_qemu_tables.h`, and tables the test builds with checksums made right or wrong. Rows check the RSDP search; each field the kernel reads from the two QEMU machines; an XSDT of 64-bit entries; tables with a bad checksum or length, left out and logged; MADT overrides of each polarity and trigger and their routes, short entries, and 66 processors; the FADT's revisions and registers; PCI registers of one and two bytes, and one past offset 255 refused when no ECAM window covers bus 0; the kernel's 64 mapping windows running out; and `\_S5` packages in each encoding and malformed.
* `acpi_ec_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/acpi/ecio.c`, the embedded controller's command protocol, and runs it against a model of an EC's two ports and its 256 bytes, which takes each byte after a time of its own on a clock that the protocol's waits move on. Rows check reads and writes of an EC that takes a byte at once, in a microsecond, in 300 and in 20,000, with no byte written before the EC took the one before and both buffers empty after; queries in order, SCI_EVT cleared with the last and 0 when none is left; an EC whose input buffer stays full, one that takes the command and nothing more, and one that never puts a byte out, each timing out after the step's limit with nothing written past it and the value left alone; an EC slower than the poll but within the limit, and one slower than the limit; and a limit of 0, which still reads the status once.
* `acpi_mapcache_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/acpi/mapcache.c`, the windows of physical memory that ACPICA's OS layer maps, over a model map that records each window it is asked for. Rows check that no bytes, a range that wraps, one in the last page and one past the limit give 0 and map nothing; that a first request maps the whole pages it touches and keeps its offset; that a request inside a window is found in it, with no second map; that a range across pages comes back contiguous from a window of its own; that a full array refuses a new window, marks the cache full and still serves the windows made; and that a failed map makes no window.
* `ideapad_hosttest.c` includes the IdeaPad's VPC2004 driver, `rach2/sys/acpi/ideapad.c`, without its ACPICA glue, and runs it against a model of the methods `VPCR` and `VPCW` over the controller's command and data registers, set as the Lenovo G50-70 reads, with models of the event device and of rfkill. Rows check the boot: the event device "Ideapad extra buttons" with its five keys, the radio switch turned on, `ideapad_wlan` registered as a Wi-Fi switch of no radio and the controller's Wi-Fi bit left alone, and no switch when `_CFG` has no Wi-Fi; each event bit's key in the table's order, bit 13 as `KEY_WLAN`, none for the brightness's bit 4, and the events cleared by their read; bit 9's read of the radio switch, which blocks only on a machine with a switch of its own; `ideapad_wlan`'s soft block written to the Wi-Fi bit; a controller 50 reads slow, one that never takes a command, which fails after 200 ms of waits, and one whose method fails, each with the lock given back; and every method called under the lock, a command never written over one not yet taken and no data read before its command was taken.
* `smbios_hosttest.c` runs the search for the firmware's SMBIOS table in `rach2/sys/arch/x86/smbios.c` over a few buffers at physical addresses, read through `smbios_set_memory`, each entry point with structures of a vendor of its own. The vendor read tells which was found: UEFI's SMBIOS 3 table, past 4 GiB, first; its SMBIOS one when there is no other or the other's checksum fails, and through a 32-bit system table; the one scanned for below a megabyte when the system table's signature is wrong or there is none; and none with no entry point. Rows through `smbios_set_table` check the system's maker and product name, matched as Linux's DMI_MATCH matches them, a substring of each and case-sensitively, before and after the BIOS's structure, and that a system with no product name, or no table, matches nothing.
* `power_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/power.c`, without `power_stop` and its halt, with the SMBIOS vendor reader of `smbios.c` and the board probe of `board.c`, and runs the reset and the power off against models of the I/O ports, of a PIIX4's configuration space and of the FADT's reset register, with SMBIOS tables given through `smbios_set_table`. Rows check that Insyde's system-management command goes to a firmware whose vendor is Insyde and not to SeaBIOS, a shorter name or a table with no vendor; that the vendor is found when it is the second string and not taken from another; that it is read once and kept; and that a structure longer than its table or an unterminated string gives no vendor. Other rows check the order of the reset's writes, and which `rach2.resetskip` leaves out; and that the power off writes the PIIX4's sleep word to its PM1 control register, and nothing to a PIIX4 that is not decoding, has no base or does not answer, or to a machine with none.
* `board_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/board.c`, the power controller profiles of the PIIX4, ICH9 and Lynx Point chipsets, and checks its decisions without running `board_init`. Rows check the lookup of a profile by its exact name; the PCI identities each matches, with the Lynx Point range at its edges; the power management base masked out of its register, an unprogrammed one refused; the decode bit; the SCI line, fixed at 9 or chosen by the low three bits, with the reserved and IOAPIC selections refused; the sleep word of each profile; whether a power management port answered and ACPI must be enabled; and that nothing is identified before the probe runs.
* `ioapic_hosttest.c` links the I/O APIC driver, `rach2/sys/arch/x86/ioapic.c`, the 8259's, `pic.c`, and `rach2/sys/cmdline.c`, with the I/O APIC's registers a model behind `ioapic_set_io` and the 8259's mask registers behind `inb` and `outb`. Its rows mask the routed lines and unmask them, with entries in the compatibility format and remapped: only the lines that were unmasked come back, every entry bit for bit as it was, and each edge line's vector, never a level line's, is raised once by a self-interrupt. Others mask the 8259 pair and give its masks back, and keep it masked once it is retired; and read `maxcpus=` as Linux's `get_option` does, so that `0`, `00`, `0x0`, `08`, a value with no digit, 2^32 and a 0 of 33 bytes keep the I/O APIC off and `1`, `010`, `0x10`, `-1` and an empty value do not; every occurrence is read, as Linux runs `maxcpus()` for each, so a 0 before or after a 4 keeps the I/O APIC off, and the last occurrence that gives a value is the count. The line is split as Linux's `next_arg` splits it: the quotes around a value or a whole word are dropped, so `maxcpus="0"` and `"maxcpus=0"` keep the I/O APIC off and `maxcpus="4"` does not, an opening quote alone runs to the line's end, `maxcpus=""` is empty, and a quoted space ends no word; a bare `--`, quoted or not, ends the parameters as `parse_args` stops there, so a `maxcpus=0` or `noapic` after it counts for nothing, while `--=1` and `--x` are words like any other; and `kcmdline_option` reads a quoted hexadecimal value. Before `ioapic_init` a mask and an unmask touch no register and raise nothing. The boot line's `nointremap` and `intremap=` are read as Linux's `setup_nointremap` and `setup_irqremap` read them: every token in the line's order, each item by its prefix, the last `off` or `on` deciding, `nointremap` counting as `off`, and `no_x2apic_optout` kept once given; `kcmdline_list` passes over items that match no word and leaves the last alone when none does; and `ioapic_remappable` answers from the DMAR table's flags, remapping offered and the x2APIC opt-out, with those words, `noapic` and `maxcpus=0`, on the x86-64 build and is 0 on any other. `noapic=1` keeps the I/O APIC off too, as Linux's `do_early_param` runs `parse_noapic` for any value. The lookups of a word are checked on their own: `kcmdline_has` matches a flag's whole word with no value and a `name=value` flag's exact value, quoted or not, so `oops=panic` and `oops="panic"` match and `oops=panicx` does not; `kcmdline_early` matches a name with any value, so `noapic=1` and `nox2apic=0` are `noapic` and `nox2apic`; `kcmdline_setup` matches a word by its first bytes, as Linux's `obsolete_checksetup`, so `nomodeset=1`, `nomodesetx` and `nowatchdogs` match and `nomode` does not; each compares names with `-` and `_` alike, so `intel-iommu=off` is `intel_iommu=off`, and none reads past a bare `--`.
* `lapic_hosttest.c` runs the local APIC driver, `rach2/sys/arch/x86/lapic.c`, against a model of the APIC page and of the base address register, with `cpuid`'s answers set by each row, and checks what the driver wrote. Rows check an APIC in virtual-wire mode, enabled with its identity read, the task priority cleared, `LINT1` and a correct `LINT0` left alone, and the spurious register enabled with vector 255 and without the firmware's suppression of the broadcast end-of-interrupt; a wrong `LINT0` corrected, after the software enable; an APIC disabled in its register enabled by one write; no APIC, no model-specific registers, a bad base and `rach2.nomsi`, each refused with nothing written; and the end-of-interrupt, `lapic_pending`, `lapic_repair_lint0` and the spurious handler's count. The model keeps the x2APIC's MSRs in the page's array and counts a page access in x2APIC mode, an MSR access outside it and a move from x2APIC to xAPIC mode that skips the disabled state, which resets the registers and clears the request, in-service and trigger-mode registers; while interrupts are on it takes each vector the request register holds, the end of a level one clearing its line's remote IRR. Its x2APIC rows check the mode under a hypervisor, with the identity read whole, the end-of-interrupt, an interprocessor interrupt as one write of the command register with the destination in its high half, a message to itself and the timer through MSRs; xAPIC mode kept on bare metal, under `nox2apic`, under `noapic` or `maxcpus=0`, without the processor's bit, and under a hypervisor for an identity of 255, which remapping may still move; a firmware's x2APIC mode kept until remapping's format is known, then kept by remapping in x2APIC format and left through the disabled state without it, a level interrupt waiting there taken and ended first with the 8259 and the I/O APIC's lines masked across the leave and a message that came in the last moment sent again, the timer's and the error's entries masked across it and written back, a tick dropped by the masked timer, whose count the model reloads at the first access after the mask, raised once, none for a timer that did not reload or was masked before, and a one-shot timer started again from the count it had left, a panic with an identity past 254, and settled at once where no remapping can come, left or refused; a firmware's mode kept when locked and under a hypervisor, there with an identity past 254 too, with or without remapping; `nox2apic`, which leaves a firmware's mode at once and takes no effect on a locked one or one with an identity past 254; the move interrupt remapping makes, every register kept, the request and trigger-mode registers among them; and processors started after it.
* `kcpu_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/kcpu.c` whole over a modelled `cpuid` and the two MSRs it reads. Rows check the x2APIC identity from leaf 0xB and from leaf 1 where leaf 0xB is absent or counts no processor; a hypervisor that allows x2APIC mode unremapped, as Linux 6.12.115's `x2apic_available` answers: KVM at 0x40000000 and at 0x40000100 behind a Hyper-V leaf, Hyper-V with both its MSRs and not without either or with too few leaves, Xen with two leaves and not with one, Xen at 0x40000100 behind a Hyper-V leaf, and none for VMware, for a signature one byte off, on bare metal or without the processor's x2APIC bit; and a firmware's lock of x2APIC mode, which takes leaf 7's bit, IA32_ARCH_CAPABILITIES' and IA32_XAPIC_DISABLE_STATUS' together. Each row fails when a leaf, a bound, the registers' order or the bytes' order is wrong.
* `pmu_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/pmu.c`, the hard-lockup detector's performance counter, and runs it against a model of the counter's model-specific registers, in which a register the processor lacks, a write of a read-only one, reserved bits or bits past the counter's width is a #GP, and a legacy write of IA32_PMC0 is sign-extended from bit 31. The nine processors are the Lenovo's Core i3-4030U, a Haswell with the architectural monitor of version 3, three fixed counters and full-width writes, and one without full-width writes, both of which take fixed counter 1; a hypervisor's version 3 without fixed counters, a monitor of version 1 and AMD's core counters, which take general counter 0; AMD's family 15h, which takes core counter 2; and a hypervisor's counter that never moves, one without the cycles event and one without model-specific registers, which take none. Each row starts with general counter 1 counting cycles with its interrupt on, and fixed counter 0 counting where there is one, as a firmware may leave them, and checks the probe's line, the counter it takes and the clock, and that counter 1 stopped; then the detector's counter enabled for 10 seconds: its control written last, fixed counter 1's field 0xB0 or the event select of the cycles event with its interrupt, its counter alone in the global control, bit 33 or bit 0, which leaves the firmware's fixed counter 0 stopped, the APIC's entry an unmasked NMI, the NMIs due at 10 and 20 seconds of a 25-second run, a period wider than 31 bits counted in loads, the entry unmasked again after each NMI and after a late one, an NMI of another source refused, AMD's window for a late NMI opened after the overflow's callback, and the counter stopped and silent after the disable with its entry masked. Other rows start a processor (`platform_pmu_cpu_setup`) with LBR left on: IA32_DEBUGCTL gets FREEZE_WHILE_SMM, LBR kept, on the Haswell and the hypervisor's version 3, whose IA32_PERF_CAPABILITIES offer it, and nothing is written on the chip without that bit, the version 1 monitor, AMD's and the one without MSRs, where the bit is reserved and its write a #GP.
* `refclock_hosttest.c` runs the reference counter of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/refclock.c` over an HPET whose registers are an array given through `refclock_set_registers` and a PM timer that moves a count at each read until a row stops it. Rows check that the firmware's armed comparators are found quiet with their raised status cleared, and the legacy routing off; that an HPET whose counter stands still gives way to the PM timer, and that neither is taken when both stand still; the counts a wait of 1, 200 or 1000 microseconds takes; and that a wait on a counter that stops ends.
* `udelay_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/udelay.c` and runs `k_udelay` against a model of the 8254's channel 0 in mode 3: a count that falls at every port access and reloads at zero, a latch kept for two reads, and live reads a byte at a time. Rows check that a wait lets its counts pass, across the reload too; that every sample is taken with interrupts off; and that an interrupt handler that latches the counter itself cannot tear a sample between the wait's latch and its reads.
* `cycleus_hosttest.c` reuses the calibration fixture without its rows and compares the lock counters' cycle-to-microsecond conversion with native division, for an absent rate, fractional ticks, counts above 32 bits and a full-width divisor.
* `timercal_hosttest.c` includes `refclock.c`, `pit.c`, `udelay.c` and `apictimer.c` of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/` and runs the three calibrations of a timer's rate: the local APIC timer's against the PM timer, and against the 8254 where there is no reference counter, and the TSC's against the 8254. Every device is read at a model clock of nanoseconds that each access moves on: a 24-bit PM timer that wraps during the measurement, channel 0 of the 8254 in mode 3, the APIC timer in one-shot mode at 1 GHz and at 8.333 MHz, and a TSC of 2.893417 GHz whose low half wraps. Rows stop the processor once, for 3 ms, 23 ms or 700 ms, before each access near the start, the end and each read of the APIC timer or the TSC, and before every 37th elsewhere, and require each result within one part in 400 of the rate the model gives. Other rows check that `refclock_scale` gives all ones for a quotient past 32 bits or a divisor of 0, which a stop longer than the reference's wrap can ask of it; that a reference that stops gives no calibration; that the TSC is measured only where it runs at one rate and no platform counter keeps time; and, at 1 GHz, that the first `timer_wake_at` turns the APIC timer to one-shot counting to the nearer of the wake and the next tick, kept at its absolute time, that an interrupt before either programs the rest, that a wake armed while one is armed keeps the earlier, that a late tick keeps the ticks' phase by moving the tick forward whole ticks, that an interrupt of the tick's vector before the tick's time is not a tick, that an interrupt of the wake's vector at the tick's time programs one count to the tick's vector and the tick then comes once, that a tick late by most of a tick moves the next tick only past the clock and an interrupt of the tick's vector a little before its time is that tick, that a tick 5000 ticks behind starts again a tick from now, that a tick raised and not yet taken at the switch to one-shot is counted once, that a stop between the clock's reading and the count's read at the switch leaves the ticks' phase, and that times asked during a pass (`timer_wake_hold`), an inner pass's too, program the timer once, for the earliest, at the outermost release (`hr_rows`). Rows of KVM's clock pages (`kvm_rows`) check that KVM's clock, a count a nanosecond, is the reference where the pages are kept; that a withdrawn stable bit hands the clock to the PM timer and the bit set again hands it back, each without a step, on a processor whose TSC is a second ahead and with a stop of 100 ms inside the hand-over; that a hand-over 65 s late counts the bound's 60 s, waits, and counts the other 5 s at the next pass; and that with no platform counter a withdrawn bit keeps KVM's clock and a TSC ahead counts no tick. No row runs the router's return from a vector-32 interrupt that `apic_hr_entry` refuses, which sets `tick_unlocked` so that `isr_leave` pops no frame of the call it interrupted: `rach2/sys/arch/x86/isr.c` holds x86 inline assembly, the FPU status reads of its exception handlers, which the Mac's arm64 compiler refuses, so no host test includes it, and a fixture that extracted `_irq_router` and `isr_leave` would stand in for some thirty functions and variables, the big lock's frame record among them, and check its own stand-ins rather than the lock. That return is checked by reading.
* `ps2mouse_hosttest.c` includes the PS/2 mouse driver `rach2/sys/arch/x86/ps2mouse.c`, with the 8042's waits of `i8042.c`, and runs its bring-up, its IRQ 12 handler and the mouse's commands against a model of the 8042 and the mouse behind it. The model's output buffer is a queue of bytes, each with its status, and its data port counts its reads; it keeps every port write in order, keeps the configuration byte, answers the commands 0xA8, 0xA7, 0x20, 0x60 and 0xD4, and passes a byte to the mouse, which answers only while its clock is enabled. Rows check that a keyboard byte, before a packet and in the middle of one, and an empty buffer leave the data port unread and the packet as it was; that a mouse byte is read and advances the packet; that three make one packet with its button and signed movements, Y turned to Linux's REL_Y; that an IntelliMouse's fourth byte is its wheel's turn and an Explorer's a 4-bit turn with the side and extra buttons, a 6-bit vertical or horizontal turn that keeps those buttons held, as Linux's psmouse reads them; that a first byte without bit 3 and a packet with an overflow bit are dropped; that a command takes as its reply only a byte marked as the mouse's, dropping a key that comes first, and fails after three tries when only a key comes; and that the reset reads its three bytes past the keys pressed between them. Rows of the bring-up check, on a working controller with bytes the firmware left, the drain, the writes in order, the configuration byte written back with IRQ 12 and the aux clock enabled, the mouse reporting, the handler on vector 44 registered with interrupts off, no port touched with interrupts on, and interrupts on at the return, with the plain mouse's IntelliMouse knock unanswered, rate 100, resolution 3 and 1:1 scaling sent, and the event device `PS/2 Generic Mouse` registered; that a mouse that answers the knocks as QEMU's does is `ImPS/2 Generic Wheel Mouse` or, sent the rates of the horizontal wheel, `ImExPS/2 Generic Explorer Mouse`, with four-byte packets and Linux's product numbers; and that a stuck output buffer, a stuck input buffer, a lost configuration byte and a lost ACK of the enable each fail it with interrupts on and no handler, while a reset or a default left unanswered, and a resend of the enable, do not. The probes for a Kensington ThinkingMouse and a Genius NetMouse, which psmouse makes before the knocks, find none, and so does the probe for a Synaptics touchpad, since the test answers it for `synaptics.c`.
* `synaptics_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/synaptics.c` with `ps2mouse.c` and `i8042.c` and links `rach2/sys/evdev.c` and `rach2/sys/mouse.c`, and brings up a model of the 8042 with the Lenovo's Synaptics touchpad behind it, firmware 8.1 with an image sensor, whose replies give the identity, capabilities and coordinates Linux logged for it and a resolution of 42 and 59 units a millimetre, which the log does not give. The model takes four resolutions as a request's byte, which a status request answers as a query and a sample rate of 0x14 or 0xC8 takes as the mode or the advanced gesture mode, and can be a plain mouse, leave a query unanswered or refuse the mode. Rows check the commands in Linux's order, the mode byte 0xC5, the queried limits, no port touched with interrupts on; the event device's name, place, numbers, the bits of its properties, types, keys and axes printed as /proc/bus/input/devices prints them and compared with what Linux gives this pad, each axis's limits and EVIOCGMTSLOTS; the events of one finger, of a second in its own slot from an advanced gesture packet, of both lifted, of a button, and of a packet after a byte out of form and after a pause; the mouse device's packets, a touch's movement from its third packet and a tap's click; the packets of the motion and buttons written to a mouse's event device, one at each SYN_REPORT after a change; and that a plain mouse is knocked for a wheel and that a pad that leaves a query unanswered or refuses the mode is set back to relative mode and named PS/2 Synaptics TouchPad.
* `alps_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/alps.c` with `ps2mouse.c` and `i8042.c` and links `rach2/sys/evdev.c`, `rach2/sys/mouse.c` and `rach2/sys/arch/x86/smbios.c`, which reads the row's System Information structure, against a model of the 8042 with an ALPS pad behind it that answers what Linux 6.12's `alps.c` sends: the E6, E7 and EC reports of the row's signature and firmware, command mode's register reads and writes in the nibble tables of versions 3 and 4, version 6's monitor mode, a version 8 pad's OTP bytes, the CS19's TrackPoint identity, and the commands that reach a DualPoint's stick through the pad. The Synaptics and Elantech probes are stubs, and rows check that Elantech's is asked only when no ALPS pad answered. Rows check that each row of Linux's `alps_model_data` takes its protocol, packet form and flags; the set-up of versions 1 and 2 byte by byte, and of versions 3 to 8 by the registers, limits and resolutions it leaves; that a version 9 pad, the CS19, a plain mouse and a pad that answered Synaptics's knock are left to the PS/2 driver, and a pad whose set-up fails is named `PS/2 ALPS GlidePoint`; each version's pad and stick devices, their names, numbers and the bits of their properties, types, keys and axes in /proc/bus/input/devices' form; and the events, slots and mouse packets of each version's packets, built from alps.rst's tables, as Linux computes them: version 1's touch, tap and tap and drag, a packet out of form and one a pause cuts, the external port's mouse; version 2's stick and its buttons shared with the pad, the Latitude D620's stick bits, a stick packet cut into the pad's and the 20 ms flush; the semi-MT contacts of versions 3 to 5 from their bitmaps and profiles; version 6's stick and the pad's buttons on it; the tracked contacts of versions 7 and 8, with their sticks; and a version 3 DualPoint's reconnect after five packets out of form in a row and after its announcement 0xAA 0x00, with the bytes of Linux's `alps_reconnect` and `psmouse_initialize`, its nodes kept, and a mouse in its place probed anew.
* `elantech_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/elantech.c` with `synaptics.c`, `ps2mouse.c` and `i8042.c`, links `evdev.c`, `mouse.c` and `smbios.c`, and brings up a model of the 8042 with an Elantech pad behind it, which answers the magic knock, Synaptics' sliced queries, version 3 and 4's 0xF8 queries and each version's register sequences as Linux's elantech.rst and elantech.c have them. Fifteen pads cover hardware versions 1 to 4: rocker and jumpy version 1 pads; version 2 pads of the old firmware, with pressure, of a queried range and of a fixed resolution; version 3 pads with a pointing stick, with CRC packets on a Gigabyte U2442, with no stick and with the sample byte whose absolute mode Linux does not use; and version 4 clickpads, an IC body 7 pad of sample byte 0x2A, a Fujitsu CELSIUS H730 with a stick, a new IC with SMBus and the firmware 0x381f17 that loses its mode at a rate. The expected commands, capabilities and events are those of Linux 6.12's own `elantech_query_info`, `elantech_set_input_params` and decoders, run over the same replies and packets. Rows check each version's commands in order, the IC body and the stick's report ID on a new IC, register 0x07 written again after the rate, the SMBIOS quirks of the H730 and the U2442; the event devices' names, places, numbers, properties, types, keys and axes as /proc/bus/input/devices prints them, each axis's limits and the stick's node; the events of every version's packets, from one finger to four, version 4's status, head and motion packets of two fingers in their own slots, the trackpoint's and the CRC forms, and packets out of form that are dropped; the stick moving the mouse device's pointer and a pause resynchronising the stream; that a Logitech mouse that answers the knock is a plain mouse; that a pad Linux will not put in absolute mode, a refused register write and a refused absolute bit each end with the pad reset and named PS/2 Elantech Touchpad, after the tries and waits Linux makes; that a read back answered late is tried again and the pad set up; and the reconnect after five packets out of form in a row and after the pad's announcement 0xAA 0x00, with the bytes of Linux's `elantech_reconnect` and `psmouse_initialize`, the pad's nodes kept, and a mouse in its place probed anew.
* `psmouse_attr_hosttest.c` includes `ps2mouse.c`, `elantech.c` and `i8042.c`, links `evdev.c`, `mouse.c` and `smbios.c`, stubs the Synaptics, ALPS and TrackPoint drivers, and keeps the files that `evdev_sysfs_serio_driver`, `evdev_sysfs_serio_files` and `sysfs_add_attr` would make and the ports `evdev_sysfs_serio_add` asks for, which it reads and writes as sysfs calls them. The auxiliary device is a mouse that answers as QEMU's, with a wheel, a ThinkingMouse's ID or a Genius mouse's status as a row gives it, and a poll that a row can leave unanswered; or elantech_hosttest.c's model cut to a version 1 pad and two version 4 clickpads, one of firmware 0x381f17; and a row can put a mouse behind a Synaptics stub's pass-through port. Rows check the five files of serio1 and of serio2, their values after the bring-up, the bytes each write of `rate` and `resolution` sends with the device disabled around it, psmouse's rate table and resolution codes and `kstrtouint`'s refusals; `resetafter`'s reconnect and 0's none, ENODEV while a reconnect is due, `resync_time`'s idle limit, the resync's disable, poll and enable and its reconnect when the poll fails, and the switch's rule for `resync_time`; each name of `protocol` matched whole, the refused names, OLPC HGPK's among them as an x86-64 kernel refuses it, the switches with their new nodes and kept names and the fallback to PS/2, and the ThinkingMouse and Genius mouse forced from another protocol, with their detection commands and resulting motion or wheel packets; the guest's rate with the pad disabled around it and its protocol switches, which keep serio2's psmouse files as its driver's; and the Elantech pad's thirteen files, a register read and written, version 4's read of register 0x07, `kstrtou8`'s refusals, a refused write, the settings, the read that fails while a reconnect is due, the switch away and back with its files, register 0x07 after the rate of firmware 0x381f17, and version 1's forced bits, against what Linux 6.12's psmouse-base.c and elantech.c do. Further rows keep replaced devices open until all 32 slots are occupied, check that a refused switch preserves the working mouse or guest, and exhaust a pad's replacement slots too; reject protocol names with an embedded NUL and suffix; remove and recreate the guest's files and serio2, keep serio2 when the guest fails its reconnect, when none answers at all and in SynRelPS/2, where no guest is probed; park a write of serio2's `rate` behind a probe of serio1 that binds a new guest, to check that it fails with ENODEV and sends the new guest nothing; and park Elantech reads and writes on the real command wait, switch protocols and resume them, also after rebinding Elantech, to check that expired callbacks send no Elantech command.
* `psmouse_synaptics_hosttest.c` includes `ps2mouse.c`, `synaptics.c`, `trackpoint.c` and `i8042.c` with synaptics_hosttest.c's model of the Lenovo's pad, and keeps the files sysfs would make as psmouse_attr_hosttest.c does. Rows run the reads and writes of the Lenovo's trial: the five files of serio1 and their values after the bring-up, SynPS/2, 80, 200, 5 and 0; the mode byte 0x85 that a rate of 40 sends and 0xC5 that 80 or more sends, with the pad disabled around it, and the 40 or 80 that `rate` reads after, as Linux's `synaptics_set_rate` keeps; `resolution`'s codes; `resetafter` and `resync_time` kept; a finger's packet moving the event device's ABS_X after each write; the switch to `bare`, which leaves the pad in relative mode as Linux's `synaptics_reset` does, and back to `synaptics`; and `synaptics-relative` as Linux's `synaptics_init_relative` sets the pad up: the mode byte 0x45, the node's name, the model 0x01b1 as its version, its left and right buttons without a middle one, its rate through the pad's own setter, `disable_gesture` read and written, a reconnect that keeps the mode and the node, the file's callback refused once the pad is back in absolute mode, and a write of it parked on the real command wait behind a switch to absolute mode, which fails with ENODEV after taking the lock and sends no mode byte.
* `trackpoint_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86/trackpoint.c` with `synaptics.c`, `ps2mouse.c` and `i8042.c`, links `rach2/sys/evdev.c` and `rach2/sys/mouse.c`, and brings up a model of the 8042 with a Synaptics touchpad, the Lenovo's with what a row turns on beside: a pass-through port, the stick's buttons wired to the pad, a clickpad, SMBus, a board ID, another model ID or late knocks. The rate 0x28 after a sliced byte sends that byte to the guest behind the port, whose replies come back in byte 1 of pass-through packets: none, a mouse that the knocks give a wheel or an Explorer's, or a TrackPoint of a kind, firmware and button count, with a RAM that its 0xE2 operations read, write, toggle and reset. Rows check the PNP IDs and firmware ID Linux's pnpacpi and i8042 make of a device's _HID and _CIDs and their match without case; every ID of Linux 6.12's `min_max_pnpid_table`, `topbuttonpad_pnp_ids`, `forcepad_pnp_ids` and `smbus_pnp_ids` brought up as Linux treats it, the boards at the limits, the ForcePad's 50 ms press and SMBus's log; for each TrackPoint kind, IBM, ALPS, Elan, NXP, JYT Synaptics and Synaptics, the bytes in the order of Linux's `psmouse_extensions` and `trackpoint_detect`, the event device's name, numbers, properties and buttons, its files, and a packet; an IBM stick of two buttons, of none, refusing the button query, and failing its reset, which writes every setting; the pass-through framing; a plain, wheel and Explorer mouse behind the port and their packets; the stick's buttons sent out of band; the files read and written, with `kstrtou8`'s and Linux 6.12's `kstrtobool`'s refusals; a write of a stick's file parked on the real command wait behind a switch of the guest to PS/2, which takes the stick's files away and keeps psmouse's, and fails with ENODEV sending nothing; and the reconnect after five bad packets in a row, with a late pad, another pad, the announcement 0xAA 0x00 of the pad, a mouse or the guest, and the guest's reconnect, failure and probe anew, against the bytes Linux's `synaptics_reconnect`, `__psmouse_reconnect` and `trackpoint_reconnect` send.
* `ps2kbd_hosttest.c` includes the PS/2 keyboard driver `rach2/sys/arch/x86/ps2kbd.c`, with `i8042.c`, and runs the IRQ 1 handler, which gives the set-1 decoder each byte that does not answer the LED command, and the LED command against the same model of the 8042, which keeps the bytes written to the data port. Rows check that a mouse byte, before a key and after one, and an empty buffer leave the data port unread and give the input layer nothing; and that a keyboard byte is read and given on as it came. LED rows step the tick at set ticks and answer through the handler: no command before `kbd_init`; 0xED at the first tick, the LED byte only after its ACK, and the ACKs and RESENDs consumed while a byte is out and given on as keys otherwise, with a key between them still a key; a change of the LEDs wanted 50 ms after the last command and not before; a byte written again after a RESEND, and after 100 ms without an answer, and the command given up with one warning after three writes, not tried again for the same LEDs; and nothing written while the input buffer is full, at the tick or after an ACK, the byte going out at the next tick. The rows count ticks at the 1000 Hz of the test's `host-cflags:` line, and `ps2kbd250_hosttest.c` runs them at the shipped 250 Hz, where the gap of 50 ms is 13 ticks, the wait of 100 ms 25 and the rows' unit of 10 ms rounds up, as Linux's `msecs_to_jiffies` rounds at HZ=250.
* `keymap_keytable_hosttest.c` runs every case of `keytable.txt` through `rach2/sys/keymap.c` with its built-in US keymap: each case's events go to `keymap_key` from a fresh state, with Num Lock and Caps Lock as its modes say, and the results to `keymap_encode` under its cursor and keypad modes, and the bytes must be the table's; a local case must give a scrollback action and send nothing. A tty case passes the bytes through a model of the line discipline with Linux's default control characters: ICRNL, the signals of ISIG, and ICANON's whole lines with VERASE.
* `keytable_hosttest.c` reads `keytable.txt` through `keytable.c`. It checks that the table reads without a refusal, names each of the 105 keys in some case, and has results that the reader quotes and reads back unchanged; that a key line is 64 cases, with the modifiers pressed in the order Shift, Ctrl and Alt, both sides of a class left first, and released in reverse; that a press line with one class is 3 cases; and that each malformed line is refused with its line number.
* `libtsm_keys_hosttest.c` compiles libtsm's sources from the patched tree that the host build of libtsm extracts and the build cache keeps, `build/host/libtsm-4.8.0/`, and gives a vte over an 80x24 screen sequences and keys, comparing the bytes it sends back. Rows check the Kitty keyboard protocol's query, push, pop and set, the flags it drops, the eviction of the oldest of nine pushes, the separate stacks of the main and the alternate screen and their reset by RIS, and a query split byte by byte; that `CSI = c` no longer answers as DA1; that plain `CSI s` and `CSI u` save and restore the cursor while `CSI ? s`, `CSI s` under DECLRMM, `CSI 2 u` and the protocol's forms do not, that the main and the alternate screen each keep a saved cursor, and that DECRC leaves autowrap as it is; xterm's bytes from `keytable.txt` for F1 to F12, the editing and cursor keys in both cursor key modes, Escape, Tab, Enter and Backspace, Control with each text key, and the keypad with Num Lock off and on in both keypad modes, under all eight combinations of Shift, Control and Alt; and the protocol's keys under flags 1, 2 and 4: its specification's examples (ctrl+shift+a as `CSI 97;6u`, ctrl+shift+tab as `CSI 9;6u`, Russian ctrl+C as `CSI 1089::99;5u`), Enter, Tab and Backspace kept as bytes and without release, text keys as text, the lock modifiers, repeat and release events with and without flag 1, the shifted and base layout keys, and the legacy encoding back after a pop.
* `libtsm_probes_hosttest.c` compiles libtsm's sources from the same patched tree and checks `0021-probes-modes-strings.patch` over an 80x24 screen, comparing the bytes the vte sends back, what its clipboard, OSC and LED callbacks receive and the cells it leaves. Rows check mode 2026's hold, set, reset, ended by `tsm_vte_end_sync_hold`, RIS and DECSTR; mode 1004's `CSI I` and `CSI O` only while it is set; DA1, DECID, DA2 and DSR 5; CPR and DECXCPR at home, waiting to wrap and in origin mode; OSC 10 and 11 with BEL and with ST; XTVERSION to `CSI > q` and `CSI > 0 q` and not `CSI > 1 q`, and DECLL only without a marker; DECRQM for every ANSI and DEC mode the parser takes, set and reset, the fixed ones, mode 2027 and an unknown mode as not recognized; a group of probes ended by DA1, with an unknown OSC and XTGETTCAP among them, answered in order whole and in pieces down to a byte; OSC 52 with BEL and ST, its query, an empty payload, none, CAN and NEL cancelling it, 1 MiB of text in base64 in reads of 4096 bytes, byte by byte, and to the OSC callback when no clipboard callback is set; and unknown strings and strings of 3 MiB for OSC, DCS, SOS, PM and APC, with ESC and C1 introducers, whole and split, which leave only the `Z` written after them, and the strings taken whole after an oversized one.
* `rtc_hosttest.c` runs the CMOS clock of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/cmos.c`, the clock of `rach2/sys/clock.c` and the PIT of `rach2/sys/arch/x86/pit.c` with port stubs for the hardware, built with `-DRACH2_HOST_CPU` so that `arch_cpu_id` returns the test's `host_cpu`. Rows check `rtc_decode` against seconds worked out by hand: BCD and binary, the 12-hour clock, leap years, and the fields and status bits of a clock with no battery, each refused; `rtc_snapshots_agree` and the boot's use of a pair of reads; `rtc_time_from_anchor` and the way back, `rtc_time_of_seconds`; the calendar past its first 400-year cycle and at its last second, 2147483647-12-31 23:59:59, one second past which is refused; the clock written in BCD and read back; real time and time since boot within a tick and across the counter's wrap; setting the clock; the microsecond clock read on the boot processor and on another, never earlier than a reading before it; and the coarse clocks across the stop of a model of the vDSO's clock whose coarse base is 5 ms ahead of the tick's, never earlier after the stop than before it.
* `vclock_hosttest.c` includes `rach2/sys/arch/x86_64/vclock.c`, the x86-64 vDSO's clock, built for x86-64 with `RACH2_HOST_X86_64`, over a model machine: KVM's clock in nanoseconds, one clock on every processor, a TSC of 2 GHz on the boot processor that a row may hold back while KVM's clock moves on, as KVM_SET_CLOCK may, a running processor whose TSC may be ahead of it and whose page may read KVM's clock some nanoseconds behind, and separate stable bits for the boot processor's page and the running processor's. Rows check that a timer interrupt reading the clock at each step of the data page's build, before and after the frame is cleared, reads nothing and waits on no count of the uncleared frame; that KVM moving its clock a second on with the stable bit kept and the TSC still moves the vDSO's reading a second on, since the page counts KVM's clock, and the tick carries the base with it; that a tick whose own page withdrew its bit while the boot processor's keeps it leaves the clock running, carried by KVM's clock; that once KVM withdraws the boot processor's stable bit, on a processor whose TSC is a second ahead of the one the page's base was read on, the system call reads the clock carried on by KVM's clock and pairs no TSC with it, and the vDSO's reading (`vclock_pass`) reads nothing, where without the check it reads the TSC a second ahead; that a setting of real time that leaves it where it was carries the monotonic and raw bases to their moment by KVM's clock and not by that TSC; that a reading of KVM's clock 100 ns behind an earlier one, with the page's bit and without it, moves no base back, so that the reading after it counts no time twice; that the stop, decided by the boot processor's page alone, then raises clock.c's floors to its moment by KVM's clock and no further, at or past every reading given; that the stop interrupts every other processor once, after the generation went up, and the start none; that a start the tick decided on, overtaken by a new withdrawal, leaves the clock stopped and the floors where they were; that the clock started again while the tick's clock is still behind the stop's time reads the stop's time first and goes on from it at KVM's rate; that, a tick later, a reading without the boot processor's bit 100 ns past the tick's base and a second one of KVM's clock 50 ns behind it read no earlier than the first, and that once the bit is back, before any tick, the boot processor's page reading KVM's clock 50 ns behind the first reading, the vDSO's reading and the kernel's read no earlier than it;
